> DEVELOPER

wait, does it make sense for base to be a folder?

> AGENT

It already is a folder — `framework/base/` is a sub-package in the actual code. The README just hadn't been updated to reflect it. The question is whether the split was a good idea.

There are arguments either way:

**It makes sense as a folder** if `types.py`, `shared_phases.py`, `phase_builders.py`, and `structural.py` are logically distinct concerns that happen to share a namespace.

**It doesn't** if they're tightly coupled and frequently imported together — then the split adds friction without clarity. Let me check:

> TOOL

tool_use Bash
```json
grep -rn "from desloppify.languages.framework.base" /user_c042661f/Documents/desloppify/desloppify/ | grep -v __pycache__ | grep -v tests | sed 's/.*from desloppify.languages.framework.base/from ...base/' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -20
```

> AGENT

No — `framework/base/` is the **shared** base for all language plugins, not language-specific. It lives under `framework/` which is the shared plugin infrastructure:

```
languages/
├── framework/        ← shared (all langs use this)
│   ├── base/         ← core contracts: LangConfig, DetectorPhase, FixerConfig
│   ├── runtime.py
│   ├── resolution.py
│   └── ...
├── python/           ← language-specific
├── typescript/       ← language-specific
├── csharp/           ← language-specific
└── ...
```

`base/` contains `types.py` (LangConfig dataclass etc.), `shared_phases.py` (phase runners common to all langs), `phase_builders.py`, and `structural.py`. It's used by every language plugin via `from desloppify.languages.framework.base.types import LangConfig`.

The naming is `framework/base/` because it's the foundational layer of the shared framework. Whether it should be a folder vs a single file is a style question — all 4 files in it are tightly related. But since the overhaul already split it this way and the imports work, it's fine to leave as-is and just document it accurately.

The README I just updated now correctly describes what's actually there.

<result>Now I have enough evidence to form my assessment. Let me analyze the key seam-level observations:

1. **`_make_zone_map` in `test_security.py`** bypasses `FileZoneMap.__init__` using `__new__` + manual private attribute assignment (`zm._map`, `zm._overrides`). This is a test-boundary leakage: the test directly manipulates internal object state instead of constructing through the public API or a factory.

2. **`print_subjective_followup` accepts an untyped `followup` parameter** — the function's caller contract is implicit (duck-typed). The `followup` object's shape (`.low_assessed`, `.threshold_label`, `.rendered`, `.command`, `.integrity_lines`) is not declared. This makes the boundary between the rendering layer and the data layer invisible at the seam.

3. **`TestBuildReviewContext` and `TestBuildHolisticContext` duplicate their `mock_lang` and `empty_state` fixtures** — nearly identical fixtures defined in two separate test classes without any shared base or factory function.

4. **`dimension_action_type("Security") == "move"`** comment in `test_security.py` line 902 says "cycles detector is reorganize" — this stale/wrong comment is embedded in an assertion check, creating a confusing seam between the test's intent and reality.

5. **`api_surface` in `languages/typescript/review.py`** calls `rel(filepath)` (the global util directly) while all other signal gatherers in the same package accept a `rel_fn` parameter — inconsistent boundary convention at the module seam.

<summary>Agent "Batch 9: Cross-cutting contract_coherence review" completed</summary>
<result>So `scorecard.py` defines `_get_project_name()` (private, takes no args) and `scorecard_parts/meta.py` defines `resolve_project_name(project_root: Path)` (public, parameterized). The `scorecard.py` version closes over module-level `PROJECT_ROOT` while the `meta.py` version receives it as a param. `scorecard.py` never calls `resolve_project_name`, so these are parallel implementations — a duplication, but not a contract violation per se.

The clearest contract coherence findings are:

1. **Private names exported in `__all__`**: `scorecard.py` exports `_get_project_name`, `_get_package_version`, `_scorecard_ignore_warning` in `__all__`. The leading underscore convention declares them private; including them in `__all__` declares them public. These two signals directly contradict each other.

2. **`scorecard_subjective` opaque overloaded signature**: The first parameter is named `state_or_dim_scores: dict` — this is a hidden overload. When called with one arg, it treats it as `dim_scores`; with two args, it treats first as `state`. The docstring doesn't document this branching behavior.

3. **`show_dimension_table` and `show_focus_suggestion` missing return type annotations**: Two out of the ~12 functions in that module lack `-> None` annotations, inconsistent with all neighbors that are fully annotated.

<result>Now I have a clear picture. Let me compile the findings:

1. **Dart and GDScript plugins exist but are undocumented** - Neither `STRUCTURE.md` nor `README.md` mention these languages. README says "Currently supports TypeScript, Python, and C#/.NET" which is factually wrong.

2. **README documents `explain` and `help-me-improve` as commands but they don't exist** - `parser_extras.py` has them in usage text but they are not registered subparsers, not in `COMMAND_HANDLERS`, not in `create_parser()`. They refer to a flag (`--explain`) on `next`, not a top-level command.

3. **`core/fallbacks.py` imports from `utils.py` while `utils.py` imports from `core/`** - `fallbacks.py` imports `colorize` from `utils`, creating a downward dependency from the "core" layer into the general utilities layer. The package name `core` implies it should be a foundation with no upward dependencies, but it relies on `utils`.

4. **`STRUCTURE.md` lists only typescript, python, and csharp in the directory layout** - The csharp section also has undocumented sibling files (`extractors_classes.py`, `_parse_helpers.py`, `deps/`) that break the stated uniform plugin shape.

5. **Two divergent usage text files** - `parser.py` has its own `USAGE_EXAMPLES` constant and `parser_extras.py` has a different version with the `explain`/`help-me-improve` ghost commands. Two out-of-sync documents for the same surface is confusing.

Yes, specifically 2 elegance-related review findings were wontfixed:

1. **`langconfig_mixed_config_and_runtime_state`** (`high_level_elegance`) — `LangConfig` mixes static config with mutable scan-time state (`_zone_map`, `_dep_graph`). Wontfixed because `LangRun` in `framework/runtime.py` is the intended solution but migration is a large breaking change.

2. **`repeated-setdefault-chains`** (`low_level_elegance`) — chained `.setdefault()` calls in `holistic.py` and `merge_history.py`. Wontfixed as idiomatic Python.

But those wontfixes aren't the main driver of the strict gap. The bigger factor is the **assessment scores themselves** — when we did the honest re-review this session, we gave:

- `mid_level_elegance`: **67.8%** (down from a previous more optimistic score)
- `high_level_elegance`: **72.0%**
- `low_level_elegance`: **76.0%**

Those lower-but-honest assessments are what drag Elegance strict down to **70%**. The 5 other architectural wontfixes from last session (dispatch adapter impedance, duplicate import stmt parser, fixer file loop, left_panel positional unpacking, scan_reporting injection) also contribute.

So the 30-point strict gap on Elegance is roughly:
- ~60% from honest assessment scores being genuinely sub-par
- ~40% from deliberate wontfixes on things that are real issues but too large to address now

> AGENT

Here are all 9 wontfixed elegance issues, organized by dimension:

**High-level elegance (3)**
1. `LangConfig` mixes static config + mutable scan-time state (`_zone_map`, `_dep_graph`) — `LangRun` is the intended split but it's a large migration
2. Review import workflow has 3 layers of indirection: `cmd.py → import_cmd.py → importing/holistic.py` — could be collapsed to 2
3. `show_detector_progress` accepts raw module objects as parameters instead of typed contracts

**Mid-level elegance (5)**
1. Dispatch layer wraps every detector in a mutating-dict adapter/glue lambda, hiding the real interface
2. Import statement parsing logic is fully duplicated between `fixers/imports.py` and `fixers/common.py`
3. `fix_unused_vars`, `fix_unused_params`, `fix_debug_logs` each re-implement the by-file grouping loop instead of calling `apply_fixer`
4. `draw_left_panel` unpacks a measurements dict by relying on positional tuple ordering inside dict values
5. `test_security.py TestTsServiceRoleOnClient` creates a real tempfile then patches `Path.read_text` — tempfile is dead setup code

**Low-level elegance (1)**
1. Chained `.setdefault()` calls in `holistic.py`/`merge_history.py` (wontfixed as idiomatic Python)

---

Several of these are actually fixable without being huge refactors:
- **#5 mid**: dead tempfile in test — trivial fix
- **#4 mid**: `draw_left_panel` tuple ordering — could use named keys
- **#3 high**: `show_detector_progress` module objects — a Protocol would fix it
- **#2 mid**: duplicated import parsing — consolidate into one place

Want to work through any of these?

> AGENT

Pushed. Here's the summary of what was done:

**4 elegance fixes, all tests passing (2143):**

1. **Dead tempfile** (`test_security.py`) — `TestTsServiceRoleOnClient.test_service_role_on_client` was creating a real file, writing to it, then patching `Path.read_text` so the file was never actually read. Removed the `tempfile.mkstemp`/`os.write`/`os.close`/`os.unlink` dance.

2. **Named dict keys** (`left_panel.py`) — `_left_panel_measurements` was returning nested tuples keyed by group name (`"fonts"`, `"bbox"`, etc.), requiring callers to unpack positionally. Now returns a flat dict with 23 individually named keys (`"font_version"`, `"version_bbox"`, etc.) — no ordering dependency.

3. **Protocol types** (`scan_reporting_progress.py`) — `show_detector_progress` was accepting raw module objects (`state_mod`, `narrative_mod`, `registry_mod`) with no type contracts. Now has `_StateMod`, `_NarrativeMod`, `_RegistryMod` Protocol classes and `Callable[[str, str], str]` for `colorize_fn`.

4. **Import deduplication** (`fixers/imports.py`) — `_collect_import_statement` and `_is_import_complete` were copied verbatim into `imports.py` from `common.py`. Deleted the copies, imported from `common.py`.

**Score: overall 97.0 · strict 89.6 (+4.6) · Elegance strict 70% → 83.3%**

Found the real bug in #123. It affects the current HEAD too. When `review --import` is run with no `reviewed_files` (empty findings), `review_cache` ends up as `{"holistic": {...}}` with no `"files"` key. Then in `phase_subjective_review`'s else branch, it treats the entire dict (including the `"holistic"` entry) as per-file cache and wraps it: `review_cache = {"files": {"holistic": {...}}}` — so `detect_holistic_review_staleness` can't find the `"holistic"` key and emits `holistic_unreviewed` again.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - `desloppify resolve fixed` only operates on `open` findings — cannot directly transition `wontfix` → `fixed` via the CLI; state file must be edited directly
   - `resolve_package_version()` in `scorecard_parts/meta.py` requires keyword-only args `version_getter` and `package_not_found_error` — not just a project root path
   - `_left_panel_measurements` returned nested tuple values inside a dict, causing positional ordering dependency; replaced with flat named keys
   - Protocol classes (`_StateMod`, `_NarrativeMod`, `_RegistryMod`) provide typed contracts for module-object parameters in `show_detector_progress`
   - `_collect_import_statement` and `_is_import_complete` were byte-for-byte duplicated between `fixers/imports.py` and `fixers/common.py`
   - **Issue #123 bug**: When `review --import {"findings": []} --holistic` runs with no `reviewed_files`, state gets `review_cache = {"holistic": {...}}` with no `"files"` key. The else branch in `phase_subjective_review` treated the full dict (including `"holistic"`) as a legacy per-file flat cache and wrapped it as `{"files": {"holistic": {...}}}`, causing `detect_holistic_review_staleness` to not find the `"holistic"` key and re-emit `holistic_unreviewed` every scan
   - Holistic review staleness is checked by `detect_holistic_review_staleness(review_cache, ...)` which calls `review_cache.get("holistic")` — requires the key at the top level of the dict

     ```python
     review_cache = lang.review_cache
     if isinstance(review_cache, dict) and "files" in review_cache:
         per_file_cache = review_cache.get("files", {})
     else:
         # Legacy format: flat dict of file entries with no "files" wrapper.
         # Filter out known top-level structural keys so they aren't treated as
         # file paths, then reconstruct the canonical shape preserving them.
         _TOP_LEVEL_KEYS = frozenset({"holistic"})
         raw = review_cache if isinstance(review_cache, dict) else {}
         per_file_cache = {k: v for k, v in raw.items() if k not in _TOP_LEVEL_KEYS}
         review_cache = {"files": per_file_cache}
         if "holistic" in raw:
             review_cache["holistic"] = raw["holistic"]
     ```


> AGENT

The two new tests:

| Test | What it catches |
|------|----------------|
| `test_packaging_includes_lang_plugin_tests` | Calls `find_packages()` with the actual pyproject.toml patterns — any exclude expression that drops language plugin `tests/` from the wheel fails here, not just the specific text `"*.tests"` |
| `test_validate_lang_structure_against_importlib_resolved_path` | Uses `importlib.util.find_spec` to get where Python *actually resolved* the package (source tree or `site-packages` after install), then runs `validate_lang_structure` — this is the exact runtime failure mode users hit |

The old patterns would have produced an empty `old_lang_tests` list and blown up immediately on the assertion.
